LumiAI — live status board

The one file every session reads first and updates last. Keep it short: if it grows past two screens it stops being read. Detail lives in the design docs; this is the board.

Updated 2026-11-13 · branch main — all work commits straight to main (operator, 2026-10-02; confirmed again 2026-10-03). claude/* branches are leftovers. claude/india-build-plan-review-e84k19 was merged into main on 2026-10-03 and is now fully contained in it — safe to delete.

🧊 DESIGN FREEZE (operator via CTO, 2026-11-25)

"This is our final production design. There could be minor tweaks, but this is it. No more major redesign."

In scope:

  • printability tweaks: fewer or no extensive tree supports, through orientation, break-off gussets or in-layer flex fixes;
  • finishing the merged LV/HV carrier: low TIE ANCHORS on the deck (4 tall, a horizontal tie tunnel; every deck run zip-tied), rigid MAINS HOOKS on the mains towers (a roof tie slot + a top-edge notch), the routes (operator option A, 2026-11-25);
  • the rev 3 in-layer LATCH (cad/freecad/lumi_powerbay_latch_fork_coupon.py, 605ac631) is PROVEN: the operator's hand test held the wires well, but it is too big and slow to print for the carrier. Keep it for a long straight lane that needs a drop-in hold;
  • defect fixes found by test.

Anything else (new features, architecture changes, re-opening a settled layout) goes to the CTO as an exception, not a plan.

⛔ Do not do these

Do not print the lens plate in translucent filament Slot 3 is the translucent spool. Clear material pipes light between the towers and the beam check becomes useless.
Do not order a dimming part F1 has not been run. It is free and it decides whether the change is needed at all.
Do not fab the Creator MCPCB Wait for E1 to pick the emitter and bin.
Do not resolve a stash-pop to the stash side in cad/exports / cad/freecad/output It once reverted the tree to a 16 mm gripper ear while BUILD_STAMP read 25.4. HEAD wins.
Do not run the FreeCAD 1.1.1 binary Banned for every session (operator 2026-11-16). On the GMKtec it is ~/.local/bin/FreeCAD.1.1.1.bak; the name FreeCAD is a link to ~/.local/bin/FreeCAD-weekly-2026.11.11, the only build to run. Rule: rules/cad.md.
Do not trust exit 0 A macro exits 0 while logging === RESULT: FAIL — N thread cut(s) did not apply ===. Read the diag, not the exit code.
Do not hand-edit per-object settings in the 3MF They are regenerated from cad/print/build_lumiai_complete_3mf.py and lost on the next rebuild. Filament slots and the print profile are preserved.
Do not rebuild the 3MF without first committing painted support Support painting is not a setting — it is a paint_supports attribute on each <triangle> in 3D/Objects/object_N.model. stl parts are regenerated by mesh_xml() and lose it; keep parts are rebuilt from the donor (cad/print/lumi_wand.3mf), so only paint that is in the donor survives — paint added to a keep part in Studio is lost too. Proven 2026-10-09: a regen dropped the tube's 87 facets, the spacer's 18, the EBC cover left's 39, and the tube's enable_support. Donor holds 257, all on EBC cover right.
Do not apply a PT4115 figure to a KESHY driver Different silicon (XL4001E). Both a PT4115 dimming model and a generic 0.235 V V_REF have already been published and withdrawn.
Do not reintroduce the one-piece head cap, the one-piece end cap, or the LED alignment fixture.

⛔ Removed 2026-10-03: "Do not print from cad/exports/ — reports STALE, still a two-rail sidewall-connector wand, lumi_wand_cyl_endcap.stl still present." That regen happened (e272cd6, 10-01). The endcap STL is deleted, the stamp reads builder=freecad, and all 45 LFS exports byte-match HEAD.

🎯 THE PLAN (operator, 2026-10-07)

1. Halo + Ultra fully built and functional, in 2–3 weeks — target October. One desktop unit, one ceiling-mounted in the garage, per the marketing doc. Gates are on the 🔴 list below; BAY and REPRINT are the two that matter. 📌 The SoCal heat wave is the thing that has been throttling this — printing was night-only in a garage with no AC. It eases 2026-10-08, and 24/7 printing takes the ~22 h of long halves from four nights to about a day and a half, so REPRINT stops being the long pole.

2. Then buy the H2S. ✅ Independently recommended by Micro Center's printer specialist, 2026-10-07 — a third voice agreeing with the analysis on this board and with the H2D field reports.

3. Then a second Raspberry Pi (16 GB — see the arm-Pi RAM floor in LumiAI_Hardware_Integration.md).

4. Then Nova.

🔴 Blocked on the operator's hardware

What Why it matters
🔶 Cradle L REDESIGNED (operator 2026-11-15: "take the screen-side leg, and put the post two-thirds in with the 3 mm deeper short arm ... Go with this"; then "Keep it as is"): reprint = the TOP LOCK L + the BOSS BLOCK; portrait base X1C 31, GRIP POST X1C 32 (red) / H2S 34 (yellow), LANDSCAPE BASE H2S 33 (finger relief) unchanged — claw cradles 30.2 × 21.2, the SEAT-FIT COUPON's notch 1. BigClaw cradle — POST ON THE L, no 4th arm Cameras at the top, near the post. The post sits on the L's crossbar with its +x face 16.7 inside the leg's outer face ("two-thirds in"), its base end 10 mm onto the crossbar's front face (foot + dovetail rib slot + 2 × M3 × 16 pan self-taps). The crossbar is 3 mm deeper from the post foot to the leg corner. The leg runs on the screen side: between the jaw frame and the crossbar it rises proud of the screen plane (underside z 13.8, 1.5 over the screen; 9.2 tall; 62° ramps; two 1.5 swells for sideways stiffness), so a finger reaches the side keys under it. Top-to-bottom lock: six ridges on the boss block face grooves in the leg's +x face, 2.0 pitch, and the block's press face is set back 0.8 so the one knob pinches the leg before the frame. The teeth do not set the height: set the L on the phone first, then tighten. No 4th arm and no dovetail rail on the crossbar. The portrait base has no foot. Post: 38.5 long; both claw cradles SQUARE at both ends, 30.2 × 21.2 × 4 for the calipered bare jaw 30 × 21; the window's tip-end roof pointed (50°, self-supporting). Seat-fit coupon (seat_fit_coupon): one 8 × 75.9 bar, three square cradles marked 1 / 2 / 3; notch 1 seats on both printers; the 75.9 bar hits the claw's mechanism at notch 2 (next coupon: separate blocks or ≤ the post's length). Swing: the hung phone's side-to-side swing is handled in motion control (Creator plan §18); no second tie to the claw. Envelope: 211.7 tall incl. the post × 132.3 wide with the cased S23 Ultra, same as the last design. Gates PASS on all 79 variants (combined 250 ok, portrait-only 243 ok, landscape-only 243 ok): rear cameras least +8.0° (GalaxyA56); USB-C plug zone clear; T knob access on all 79; L STIFF 1.02 mm (limit 1.5); top-pad flex 0.68 mm at 2 g on the cased S23 Ultra (limit 0.7; the run without its swells reads 0.90); the proud run least 1.30 off the side T (GalaxyA54) and 2.00 off the jaw, boss block, knob cage and base; lock teeth home on 14 variants, crest on land on 60, between on 5, a slip caught within 1.94 (one pitch 2.0). Side keys under the run: every measured key band reached whole (32 bands), every placeholder band over ≥ 50 % of its length (47 bands, least 57 %); bite: the leg straight (no run) on the iPhone 12 Pro Max reaches 0 of 10. Claw: the quick clearance probe (every claw part against every cradle part except the post and the phone, 23 openings 30–85) gives least 3.31 mm in portrait (claw closed on the post, to the L) and 2.1 mm in landscape (to the base, unchanged; the cased S23 Ultra landscape row did not run); the full sweep (wrist against the arm / OAK-D / view, catch bracket, bolt tip, finger access, tongue) was still running at this commit. With the claw on the post its right fingertip stands 9.51–9.96 mm in front of the top T's thumb screw on 12 thin bare phones (≥ 10 on the other 67); operator: "Keep it as is". Landscape finger relief (LAND_RELIEF): the fixed jaw's foot strip cut back to x −10.4 from z 7 up for 18 mm past the foot's rim (the claw's left finger, hung landscape); base unchanged by this design. Print: the L prints flat with two supported regions (the lip over the bezel; the run's underside over the side keys, 11.5 over the bed, 10 × 46.3), strong trees. Strength (info, MPa, limit 23.3): L under hand 10 N + phone 2 g, diagonal: crossbar 4.3, leg at the tunnel 10.0; hand alone 7.3; phone at 2 g 2.8; post foot 19.6 under the 10 N hand on a corner (9.2 at 2 g), foot into the crossbar 11.4 / 5.3; top-pad stiffness by FEM 8.01 / 2.34 N/mm (the printed L 8.54 / 2.73); twist on the claw ≈ 30 N·mm (86–113 with the post on the corner). Named MIN_WALL exception: the post window's floor 1.51 over the 8 socket (not load-bearing). Open: the top T against the rear cameras on 26 variants (18 Pixels without lens positions; 8 cased iPhone Pros at 58.6–59.4° of 60) needs lens positions or a decision. Rule: a sliding dovetail that must hold a part rigid needs a clamp across it (clearance alone gives ≈ 2c / cos θ of play).
🔶 Cradle main lock = SLIDING PAWL (operator 2026-11-24, via LumiAI CTO: the flexure arm's teeth were "useless ... they'll break right away"; his go on the concept + the first-assembly pictures). Built into the cradle macro behind QUICK_RELEASE; the TEST STATION is X1C 35: CAD 62188079, book 778bac7e -- LumiAI 3DPrinter's gate PASS (0 Studio warnings, 0 floating starts, 1 h 26, 22.8 g), SIGNED OFF by LumiAI CTO 2026-11-24; installs on the DELL when Studio closes What it is: a solid 2-tooth PAWL slides in y in the carrier's pocket and locks a rack in the base's channel (pitch 4.0, depth 2.4, a 0.8 land with r 0.4 root fillets, 8° hook that pulls the pawl in under load); the spring's force goes carrier body → pawl (bearing) → one tooth → rack → base. Retention: a −x bayonet ear in an L-slot + a 1.75 filament GUIDE ROD pressed through the seat (2.5 engaged at every point of the travel) with the pawl's spring stack on it. Hard stop = the carrier body on the block's back (compression). Carrier: 45° slope over the pawl lane (no flat pocket roof), a 4.6 bridge over the spring's room, a 1.5 × 45° lead-in on the spring bore, a thumb pad; block: a snug Ø6.1 boss holds the relaxed spring; jaw: detent bumps hold the block in. Passed: the station 38 of 38 (assembly sweeps: the block past its detent, the relaxed spring through the mouth, the carrier and pawl in; one tooth 53.1 N (~5.4 kg) at 16.0 MPa ≤ 16.7; lock face engaged 1.95; release 26.5 N (~2.7 kg); tilt hold; ear, rod, bayonet, hard-stop rows with bites); the cradle's QR rows over all 79 variants 17 of 17 (jaw travel past the widened channel, mechanism, thumb/finger access, stop lug, release drop, way out); flag-off proof: the knob cradle's 320 parts identical to the previous macro. Hardware (not printed): main spring 1.0 × 8 × 15, 304, MEASURED 6 coils (6.29 N/mm calc, 17–43 N; active ~4 and solid ~6.5 UNMEASURED); two uxcell 0.5 × 4 × 5 stacked on the rod (wire 0.5 MEASURED; ~6 N/mm each calc); a 1.75 filament guide rod ~15 long. GATE before any cradle production: the main spring runs 938 MPa at the hard stop (~800 is the usual limit for 304 this size), accepted for the TEST STATION by LumiAI CTO 2026-11-24 — the operator presses it to the hard-stop length ~50 times and its free length must stay within 0.3 of 15.0. First assembly: docs/images/hardware/qr_first_assembly.gif (from the built station: block in past its detent, the spring on relaxed, the pawl + rod into the carrier -- keep the carrier upright until it is in the tunnel -- then the thumb push). Print fixes on the way to the gate: the ridge block prints upside down (flat top on the bed; on its +x face it stood on the spring boss); the station frame's channel in front of the leg filled, the jaw roof over the leg slot filled, the tunnel's +y wall buttressed over the pawl lane (Studio's floating cantilever). The 1b142fb5 T-bracket rows (owed since 11-22 before any QR cradle plate): CLOSED -- qt_rows with QUICK_RELEASE on, all 79 variants, 13 of 13 PASS on 2026-11-24 (log 11:26:13 .. 11:30:32), including its own row: the hinge LUGS as side walls, 76.0 / 76.0 mm2 above the housing across the pin's plane.
🔴 Cradle QUICK RELEASE, a CANDIDATE: the knob cradle stays the CURRENT design. The sprung path is in the cradle macro behind a switch that is OFF (present, inert, incomplete); the working TEST STATION is on X1C plate 33 only, GATED (LumiAI 3DPrinter 2026-11-17); one spring size is on order (the top flap's); H2S plates 15 and 17 HELD (no material) What the operator asked (2026-11-16): the phone must come out of the cradle fast, so the big knob and the three M2 thumb screws of the T ledges go and every hold is spring-loaded ("So you just get things out by pressing"); the L, the base and the serrated grip stay and may change where the quick release needs it. The first coupon failed in his hands (printed flexure fingers, CAD 3f7bee11, H2S 35, 2026-11-16): "it goes in but never clicks or holds"; "The ridges are not deep enough, first of all ... Secondly, the force exerted by the tab, you created this plastic spring, is not enough." Cause, the design (3dPrint): 0.35 of tooth overlap against 0.4 of leg float, 0.55 knife-edge teeth, a finger that pressed on nothing at rest; and the hold's gate measured the nominal position only, with no print allowance. That coupon, its macro and its plates are removed. His decisions since: steel springs on all three T ledges ("Plastic tabs are not going to work ... You have to go with springs"); the T ledges are captive ("the never coming out option is better"); a T ledge must touch the phone's back ("So what good is a T-ledge which is not sitting on the back of the phone?"), by a spring and not by TPU ("I don't think TPU is going to hold the phone"); the real T ledges and every serrated cradle part print in the red Kingroon HS PETG on X1C slot 2; and on 2026-11-17: "I will not be ordering the springs till I have printed the parts and looked at them myself and checked if springs will even work in them." The knob cradle stays (operator 2026-11-17): "Please remember to keep the knob, frame model, and the ledges and all the CAD work and Python scripts for that also ... until this new design is refined, we're still going to use the old design. Because the new design is not proven yet, the old design is fairly stable." So the knob, the knob frame and the screw T ledges are the current design, their plates stay released, nothing of them is removed, and one macro builds both. He ordered ONE spring size to prove the mechanism, the top flap's (304 stainless, 0.5 wire x 4 x 5, 50 pieces, due 2026-11-19); the other two sizes are not ordered. Filament pins are for quick tests only; the shipping cradle uses thin metal screws as its pins (operator 2026-11-21: "for shipping the final product, we should not use filament ... just metal screws"); not yet in the cradle macro, which still models 1.75 filament. His rule for new mechanisms: "build test cases, not just coupons, but real, small, but real test cases, and try things out." He cannot judge a force in newtons, so forces go to him as everyday weights. The design (worked out on the released solids in a working copy): Main lock: one steel spring (1.0 wire x 8 x 15) between the ridge block and a carrier whose hooked arm sits in a rack in the base, so the spring both pinches the L and grips the phone; push the tab toward the phone to lock, push it down to release. Side T ledges: a rigid leg whose 2.0 toe sits under a ridge 1.0 proud of one end wall of its pocket; a 0.5 x 4 x 5 spring presses the leg onto that wall (about 340 g at rest, 860 g pushed aside [3.4 N, 8.4 N]; calculated, 725 MPa pushed aside). It is the springs in hand (operator 2026-11-21: "I'd just go with the 5 mm springs since they work. That way we don't have to buy other ones"): the station's 5 mm host held, and the 10 mm spring's 3.8 slot let the 5 mm spring fall out. The slot is 0.8 long, the station's proven one; the cradle's side rows (spring, hold at a 30 N yank, way out, assembly) pass with it. The spring rides on the leg, pressed into a 3.9 x 2.0 seat in its end face, and its free end drops into a slot in the host that is open to the top (a groove in the L's top face, and a 4.4 notch in the fixed jaw body's top edge on the fixed side, operator 2026-11-20: fine). In: spring on the leg, hold the T pushed toward the spring, lower it, let go; out: push it toward the spring and lift. A row lowers each T with its squeezed spring 30 straight down into place and finds nothing in the way; its bite is a closed-top hole, which it fails. They have one height. Top T ledge: a PIN-SET bracket. Its leg goes up into the crossbar's pocket and one cross pin (9.0 mm of 1.75 filament, centred in a through hole, 0.7 under each face; a paperclip pushes it out) through one of FIVE holes in the leg sets its height, once per phone: 0, 1.0, 2.0, 3.0 or 4.05 down, in three columns (the crossbar and the station frame have one 1.8 grip hole per column); the rule is the tightest hole the phone still goes in. Fine settings (operator 2026-11-21, on the printed station: released, the flap was only half down on the thickest step and almost open one setting over, so it barely held): with five settings every phone pushes the flap within 1.1 of fully down; calculated (assumed coil count) at least 6.6 N on every step of the station's bar, where the three-setting design fell to about 3 N on the thin phones. The leg cannot be longer, so the five fit in 0 .. 4.05 with at least 1.05 of plastic round every hole. Station 26/26 (the frame's two 1.8 size labels dropped). Stacked flap springs (operator 2026-11-21, on the printed station: the flap grips hard only when it is pushed fully down; "you're going to need maximum flap compression or a tougher spring"; then "unless we combine two 5s and make them work like 10"): each of the flap's two pockets takes TWO 0.5 x 4 x 5 springs stacked end to end (four in all, the springs in hand), 15.5 and 9.1 from the hinge, in place of one 5 mm spring at 12.8 and 6.4; the pair acts as one 10 free, 5.0 solid spring at half the rate. A printed COUPLER joins each pair (operator 2026-11-21: the second spring could not be balanced on the first in the 5.1 pocket): a 2.9 plug pushed into each spring's 3.0 bore either side of a 3.8 collar 0.6 thick, four printed for the two pockets; free, the collar stays inside the pocket (5.6 against its 5.7), so the pocket guides the joint, and a station row checks it touches neither bracket nor flap (bite: a plug 1.0 longer reaches the flap). The collar adds 0.6 to the stack, so the bracket's housing is 3.5 deeper and the pair sits 0.9 over solid (6.5 against 5.6) flap-down; the forces are unchanged. Their 4.1 of preload keeps the force nearly flat over the flap's travel: 11.7 N fully down and 9.9 N on the worst phone (Pixel 7a, the flap 0.9 up), where one 5 mm spring per pocket gave 8.9 N and 6.6 N (calculated on assumed coil counts; 743 MPa fully down, limit 750). No 15 mm spring is needed, and one would not fit (the housing would go 5 deeper). The station (27 ok) and the top T's cradle rows on all 79 phones pass, clearances to the base, lenses and jaw included; the full cradle run was not done (operator). The bracket changes and the coupler is new. The bracket's leg broke at its root (operator 2026-11-21, on the printed station): the couplers work and the force was more, but the grip faded step by step, and only the leg hole nearest the flap gripped at all. Cause, the design (3dPrint): the bracket printed standing on its underside, so the leg's layers ran across it, 3 walls round 25 % gyroid; the flap's load (about 27 mm out from the leg) bent the leg on one layer line at its root, the leg crept and the housing sagged about 4 mm (so the hole nearest the flap acted as setting 1 and every other hole set the flap below the steps), then the leg broke off along that layer. Measured on the sagged bracket: flap rest 4.0-4.15 under the roof against 5.75 modelled, fully pressed 9.5-9.8 against 7.85; the bar's steps measured 12, 10.2, 9, 8.2, 7.8, 7.22 (modelled 11.9 .. 7.2). The grip results so far say nothing about the springs. Fix for the test (CAD 2ade714c): the bracket prints on its BACK face (the leg's back and the web's end are one flat face), so the bending runs along its layers; it gets 5 walls and tree supports in the flap's slot (which faces down that way); every station piece prints at 100 % infill (operator 2026-11-21: "make sure you have 100% infill now"). No geometry changed; station 29 ok. The top ledge WORKS on the reprint (operator 2026-11-21): with the bracket printed on its back face the grip is "pretty solid"; pushing the bar toward the flap (the way a phone would lift out) is "very difficult ... it's strong that way"; pulled along, the bar slides with some force, as intended (the flap seats the phone, the jaws and frame stop it sliding). Each step needs its own hole (the tightest the step still goes in), as designed; the thinnest two steps in the last hole are still to be tried. MAIN LOCK TEST STATION (operator 2026-11-21: "Build a test station for the main lock also ... It's a good idea to test these things before we print the whole cradle assembly"; macro lumi_bigclaw_cradle_qr_lock_station.py, CAD 51fa665c, X1C plate 35): three pieces cut from the cradle's own parts for one phone (iPhone 12 Pro Max): a FRAME (the base's channel and rack, the jaw frame's tunnel and the L's leg end, fused; prints back face down, its overhangs ~14 mm bridges), the RIDGE BLOCK and the CARRIER with its flexure arm and tab. No spring: the lock's 1.0 x 8 x 15 is not in hand, so it tests the catch by hand: the cartridge slides in from the open end with the tab pressed; pushed toward the leg the arm clicks over the rack; pulled back the hooked teeth hold; a full press on the tab (1.5) frees it, a quarter press does not. The clamp force waits for the spring. 12/12 rows PASS with bites; renders docs/images/hardware/phone_cradle_qr_lock_station_{assembled,exploded}.png. Red PETG, 100 % infill, no supports, 10 mm brim; sliced by LumiAI 3DPrinter: 1 h 02 min, 18.2 g, both 14 mm roofs bridge clean. SPRING AXIS LOWERED 9.5 -> 7.6, BORE A FULL TEARDROP (operator 2026-11-23: "Go ahead, lower the spring axis"; CAD e090383e): X1C 35 did not slice clean in Studio -- the carrier's spring bore had a flat top, a 6.6 x 3.2 ceiling at print z 18 that no support reaches (supports off: floating cantilever; on, everywhere: 1 overhang region that could not get support), and no orientation is better. At 7.6 the bore is a full 50 deg teardrop: walls 2.10 above, 3.00 below, 2.40 a side (measured on the solid); the block's seat follows. New rows, each with its bite: SPRING LINE (the axis 1.20 inside the ridge band 6.4..14.8, so the block cannot tip; couple 111 N.mm at the hard stop, was 41), BORE WALLS, NO CEILING (0.1 mm2 looking down, was 21.0). Station 21 / 21 PASS, station ASSY 6 / 6 PASS (the spring on both seats, touching nothing), _ASSY.step re-exported; the frame unchanged. The cradle's own carrier uses the same constant (its rows not re-run: targeted rows only). X1C 35 rebuilt on it (f5a40219): gate_slice still FAILED, Studio "floating cantilever" on the carrier -- not the bore: its flexure arm, web and teeth stand 0.4 off the channel floor, the tongue 0.3, so as printed the 37 mm arm floats 0.10 over the bed, on the first layer's slice plane. Print fix (the CAD stays): the carrier is SUNK 0.10 into the bed (builder SINK_MM, verify SINK_OK); the tongue's floor clearance becomes 0.4. New verify check HOVER (one flat underside level over 5 mm2 between the first layer's slice plane and its top): it fails only X1C 35 unsunk across both books, passes sunk. Sunk, LumiAI 3DPrinter's gate_slice is CLEAN (56a3aa89: 1 h 10 min, 20.3 g, 123 layers, 0 Studio warnings, 0 supports; gate_floating 0, gate_gcode_floating 0 starts >= 3 mm): X1C 35 RELEASED again as the fit test. The cradle's own carrier will need the same sink when it reaches a plate. ⛔ was: "the spring-bore roof is a bridge he accepted (2026-11-22)". RELEASED as a fit test without the spring (operator 2026-11-22, via LumiAI 3DPrinter: print it now to see whether it works and everything fits; plate 35 "Main lock station (fit test, no spring)"; it had been held since 2026-11-21 for the spring). For the spring test: the lock spring is ordered, 304 stainless (MECCANIXITY 1.0 x 8 x 15, 10 pcs, due 2026-11-23, no coil count or rate listed); measured (free length, active coils, a rate point, solid <= 8.0) it becomes the modelled spring, any pocket change (on paper the preload 10.3 -> 10.18 clears the two floors a catalogue 304 misses) goes under its own proof, and the station is re-run; the block's seat and the carrier's bore already take the spring. Lug and tab strengthened (operator 2026-11-22, on the assembly: the block's lug "I think is too weak. If there's force on it from the spring, it's going to snap right away"; "The tab on the carrier is also too weak"): Lug (both stops; the spring's whole preload when the cartridge is out): 5.0 wide (was 3.0; the tongue's rails 3.0 -> 2.0), its faces flush with the block's relief face and back (no step at its root), 0.5 chamfers at its y roots; at the spring's hard-stop force (36.9 N) 13.9 MPa with Kt 2, limit 16.7, along its layers (the old lug 19.8, the bite). Tab: as built, a 14 tall tab on a 3.0 x 2.4 neck, bent across its layers, with nothing to stop an over-press but the arm lying on the tongue BELOW the neck: a 20 N push past what bends the arm put about 140 MPa in it. Now a STOP WALL on the carrier's end beside the tab's -y face (its face parallel to the tab at a 1.55 press, QR_TAB_STOP): the tab lands on it at 1.60, before the arm reaches the tongue (1.70), so a harder push goes into the carrier; the stem runs the tab's full 4.6 length; the neck carries only the push that bends the arm (2.0 N calculated): 8.1 MPa with Kt 2, limit 8.3 across layers (the thinnest margin here; the stem cannot thicken: the channel wall and rack are at its side). New rows qr_part_strength_rows (lug, stop order, neck), each with the old feature as its bite. Re-run on the switch-on macro, targeted: strength 3/3, stop lug 2/2, tab access 2/2, mechanism on all 79 variants 7/7, way out 2/2, release drop 2/2; the station 15/15 and its assembly 6/6; no full cradle run (operator). Close-up renders docs/images/hardware/phone_cradle_qr_lock_station_{tab_stop,lug}.png. Flexure arm and web strengthened (operator 2026-11-22: "Why'd you leave weak parts? Why didn't you run a check to see if things are weak, which are going to be pressed by springs or human beings?"; then "go ahead" on the audit's fix): the audit of every spring- or hand-loaded feature of the lock found the arm's WEB (2.5 long, joined to the tongue on one face, the locked load 2.65 off it) at 38 MPa nominal (76 with Kt 2) and the arm's ROOT at 16.5-17.6 MPa every release into a sharp corner (~35 with Kt 2). Now: web 6.0 long (QR_WEB_L), arm root 11.0 before the leg face (was 7.5: the arm 3.5 longer), arm 2.2 thick (was 2.4), a 1.5 fillet where its stressed face meets the web (QR_ARM_FILLET); the tongue, the channel and the finger groove reach 7 further back (under the jaw wall, solid plate; both cut regions measured: plain plate minus the cut, 2.0 / 2.5 of plate left). Rows qr_arm_strength_rows, at the locked load 46.6 N (the spring at the hard stop + the 3 g hung 9.7 N): root 15.3 MPa (Kt 1.3 fillet), web 13.8 (Kt 2), teeth 12.4 shared by two (one tooth alone 24.9 fails; the model bears on ~2.9); each bite the old arm (35.1 / 76.4 / 24.9). The softer arm also eases the tab's neck (5.1 MPa). Audit, passing: rack teeth, the arm as a column (251 N to buckle), the spring's end wall (~7 MPa), the rails, the stop wall, the thumb's lock push (the tab tips onto the carrier body after ~2 N). A hinged FLAP on a filament pin (8.0 mm, 1.0 blind in the front lug), two pairs of 0.5 x 4 x 5 springs, each joined by a printed coupler, under it, takes up the last 1.8 at most and bears on the phone's back: about 420 g at the least, 910 g at the most [4.1 N, 8.9 N]. With no phone the flap rests 6.7 degrees up on its heel. The cross pin is a locking peg, the hinge pin a hinge; both carry the springs' reaction in shear (calculated 6.2 MPa at a 30 N yank, 2.1 MPa at 10 N; limit 9.8). The bracket's web is 3.9 thick where the screw T's is 5.0. Station 25/25. All spring figures are calculated on assumed coil counts and are provisional until a real spring is measured; the flap springs reach 743 MPa with the flap fully down (limit 750). The lock rows are calculated for spring steel (G 79 000); a 304 spring is about 13 % softer and has not been put through them. Springs per cradle: 1.0 x 8 x 15, one (lock); 0.5 x 4 x 5, six: two for the side ledges and four for the top flap (two per pocket on a printed coupler). Calculated 4.1 to 8.9 N on the six steps (about 420 to 910 g). The hole size whose filament pin grips without a hammer is the size the cradle gets. Pins go in and come out (operator 2026-11-20, on the printed station): as printed, the hinge hole was half covered by the bracket's own web behind the rear lug and blind in the front lug, and the cross pin was blind in the frame's front wall and 0.4 under its back face, so neither pin could be put in cleanly or taken out to try another setting. The cradle macro carries the same through holes (behind its switch), and the top pad gets two 1.9 holes on the cross pin's columns so a paperclip reaches it from the front; its all-phones row runs the rod 40 past both faces with the phone out and finds nothing. In today's pocket a catalogue 304 spring misses two floors by a hair: the teeth in the grooves 9.95 x against 10, the clamp on the heaviest phone 2.9 x against 3; a preload length 0.12 shorter (10.18 instead of 10.3) would clear both on the arithmetic. (3) Release drop accepted, no detent (operator 2026-11-17): with the lock open the base and phone drop 2.6 to 23.8 mm, by phone, onto the stop lug. Taking the phone out on a base with the landscape foot: tilt the fixed side up, slide the phone about 4 mm and LIFT it off; do not slide it 20 mm sideways, it runs into the landscape foot. (4) The L's leg is 2.0 longer for the stop lug; with the shortest phone its end passes the base's bottom edge by 0.8. (5) The pin-set bracket's flat underside is 2.0 under its web; with its flap it clears the L, the phone, its cameras and buttons, the top pad, the base, the jaw frame and the post on all 79 variants (post least 4.19 away, a lens least 1.00). Cradle REAR CAMERA check now covers the T ledge (2026-11-17) The cradle macro's REAR CAMERA VIEW row never had the top T ledge or its nose pad among the parts a lens must see past. They are in it now, set at each phone's back, and the released cradle PASSES: least margin +3.6° (Pixel 8 + case, ultra-wide, at Google's published 62.9° half-angle; +6.5° at the flat 60°); the operator's Galaxy S23 Ultra +11.0° bare, +7.7° cased. Ultra-wide half-angles are per phone where the maker publishes a wider field than 120° (Pixel 7 Pro and 8: 62.9°; 8 Pro 62.8°; 9, 9 Pro, 9 Pro XL: 61.5°; every other phone 60°), and the row prints which phones it gated wider. The nine Pixels' lenses are modelled on their camera bars (lumi_phone_set.py; table, sources and tolerances in cad/freecad/lumi_phone_set_PIXEL_LENSES.md): no official Google drawing is public, so they are scaled from Google's back renders, ±0.7 mm on the 9 family, ±1.0 on the 8, 8 Pro, 7a, 8a, and the 7 and 7 Pro are weak in height (±1.5: caliper a real unit before relying on it). Before this a camera bar was sampled as ultra-wide points every 3 mm, which put the T ledge 46° inside a "lens" that does not exist. A flash is no longer a lens: the fourth element of the iPhone Pros' camera plateau is the flash; it has its own class and a bite (read as an ultra-wide it fails the row by 1.2° on the cased iPhone 14 Pro). Plates and prep: Every plate is Textured PEI on both printers. Glue stick on every plate, PLA+ included (PLA+ bed 60 °C on the X1C). Clean the PEI with soap and hot water only, no IPA. Defaults: layer 0.16, first layer 0.20, support top-Z 0.16 (verify FAILS a PETG top-Z under 1 layer); elephant-foot 0.15; X1C first layer 30 mm/s; PETG+ walls 50/200 unless a part is tuned; brim 10–12 mm, gap 0 (verify-gated; the H2S extension halves are the one 15 mm exception). Brim gate (verify, mandatory): brim only where the part needs it. An unbrimmed part with under 1000 mm² of bed contact and height / √(bed contact) ≥ 0.3 FAILS unless it is listed in NO_BRIM_OK with a reason (currently the X1C lens-tower coupons and the cartridge test coupon). Parts on a plate must be ≥ 20 mm apart, brim edge to brim edge (packer BASE_GAP 20; the packer reserves each part's brim as verify reads it, an auto brim at the profile's width: pack_brim, bite LUMI_PACK_BRIM_OFF=1 fails verify on X1C 30 at 11.3). COLLISION and that gap are judged on the parts' footprint outlines (0.5 mm shadow raster) wherever the bounding boxes flag, so a concave part's open corner is no longer a collision; the operator's waivers sit in verify's BACK_EDGE_OK / GAP_OK by plate name (X1C 27, his own layout). An auto_brim part skips the 10–12 mm width rule (Studio sizes it). Brim-auto rule (operator 2026-11-10, "when in doubt pick the Auto option for brim"): auto_brim on the base cover base + top (X1C), the Halo mic carrier and the R818 shroud (both books) and the cradle L; every other unbrimmed part keeps no_brim for a recorded reason (lens plate and end plates: operator ruling, the brim belongs to the ring; cartridge caps: operator's proven values; test coupon and lens-tower coupons: NO_BRIM_OK; bed map: a brim hides the answer; cradle bases and gap coupon: break-away prints, as the operator saved them; wand catch bracket: ~1200 mm² on the bed; ZE15-CO + SPS30 plate + strap: the operator's pins sit 20 mm apart for no brims, so a brim needs the pins moved). Blocked on HAT arrival.
🚨 NEVER AUTO-ORIENT THE RINGS Studio's auto-orient FLIPS the retaining ring and destroys the thread — caught at the machine 2026-10-10. Measured: R_ID 0.773 mm step (correct) vs R_X180 1.405 (broken), because flipping doubles bed contact (797→1581) and auto-orient optimises for exactly that. The 45° chamfer is cut on ONE flank only, so upside down the square flank faces the bed and the thread prints in open air again. 🚨 **ORIENTATIONS CHANGED 2026-10-10 (operator, at the machine): head ring R_ID, end ring R_X180, retaining ring R_X180 — and ALL THREE RINGS NOW PRINT WITH TREE SUPPORTS. Operator's words: "they print better in this orientation and yes the tree supports do not land on the thread anywhere", preview checked at the machine. The measured numbers were not wrong, they answered a different question — least UNSUPPORTED ceiling — and with supports on, a ceiling a tree reaches prints either way up. "Never auto-orient" is unchanged: Studio optimises bed contact and cannot know the thread is directional, so it must not choose. Adhesion is the brim's job (12 mm), not the orientation's.
✅ M72 THREAD CLOSED — it was ORIENTATION, never the profile Proven on hardware 2026-10-10, twice: the retaining ring printed flipped threads cleanly onto the wand halves, and the chamfered V1 coupon also threaded on. The square profile is printable. No thread chamfer (CAD 524d8f0): it cost 0.8 of the 1.4 mm bearing flank (57 % of the clamp). Ring print orientations: head R_ID, end R_X180, retaining R_X180, all three SUPPORTED (see NEVER AUTO-ORIENT THE RINGS). ⚠️ Measure printability in PRINT orientation, off the layer plane, counting only a bore NARROWING at ≥95% of angular bins. A step at ~12/180 bins is helical lead with the previous turn beneath it and prints fine; it is not an overhang.
CALIPER A real uxcell lens holder, across the ribs (max, not a valley) the Ø22.5 bore tolerates the vendor Ø22.0 being under, not over

✅ X1C lidar + TH Board Set V9 fitted 2026-10-09 (operator). Self-test passed, 6-step calibration run, and the build-plate marker read WORKED on the first attempt. The whole assembly came out in one piece — lidar, interface board and V9 toolhead board — so every electronic part in the toolhead is new and the lidar-to-board connections are factory-seated. Firmware went 01.07.05.00 → 01.12.00.00 the same day. Full write-up: X1C_Lidar_Repair_Log.md.

✅ CONFIRMED — 3 consecutive build-plate marker reads, all clean (operator, 2026-10-09): the first read straight after calibration, then plate 04 and then plate 05, each on the first attempt. That meets the gate recorded earlier today — one read plus two more — against a read that was at 100% failure the same morning. The lidar repair is closed. ⛔ The old "expect 3–5 restarts per read" working practice is dead — do not reintroduce restart-until-it-reads anywhere.

🧽 THE LENS NEEDS CLEANING — it is a CONSUMABLE MAINTENANCE ITEM, and that is now the known failure mode for a marker-read error (operator, 2026-10-10). After ~15 consecutive clean reads, one read failed. The operator cleaned the lidar lens and it reads again. Nothing else was changed.

⚠️ So a marker-read failure does NOT re-open the 2026-10-09 repair. Clean the lens first; it is a wipe, and it has now explained the only failure since the fit. Do not restart-until-it-reads, and do not treat a single failure as a regression — the 3→15+ read record stands.

🚨 CLEAN THE LENS BEFORE ANY LONG OR CRITICAL PRINT. This matters more than a nuisance error, because of what is recorded immediately below: the X1C scans the flow-dynamics calibration lines with this lidar, so a degraded read does not merely annoy — it silently miscalibrates flow and that is what produced the plate 04/05 first-layer failure. A lens dirty enough to miss the marker is dirty enough to misread those lines. The wand halves are ~5 h each and the extension halves ~6 h; wipe it first.

🔶 RE-OPENED 2026-10-11 — the NEW set fails the same way after ~15 prints. "Failed to read the build plate marker" is back on the new lidar + V9 board, firmware current. The lidar's white capture LED is bright but now flashes only briefly over the marker, and a phone light on the marker helps — the shortage is ILLUMINATION, not lens or module. Two lidars + two TH boards failing identically moves the suspect UPSTREAM: the USB-C toolhead cable (carries toolhead power, flexes every move; $12.99, in cart). Gate and full reasoning: the 2026-10-11 entry in X1C_Lidar_Repair_Log.md. Meanwhile BUILD PLATE DETECTION IS OFF (operator, 2026-10-11) and printing is fine — the one lost guard is the wrong-plate check, so keep X1C jobs set to Textured PEI / 55. Marker sheet printed as spare margin (caliper against the original before applying; matte seal if inkjet) — margin, not the repair.

ℹ️ Kept only in case of a future regression: the sole old parts left in that signal path are the toolhead cable bundle and the MC-AP board. Reseat and inspect the cable at both ends first — free — before ordering. A single degraded pair in the bundle takes out the lidar and leaves everything else printing normally; it is not true that a bad cable would stop the whole toolhead.

✅ CLOSED 2026-10-09 — the blind lidar WAS the cause of the plate 04/05 PLA first-layer failure. Plate 04 reprinted straight after the swap: layer 1 perfect on both parts, X1C inspection passed, operator's visual inspection passed. No change to temps, plate, material or settings between the failure and the success — the only variable was the lidar. Mechanism: the X1C scans the flow-dynamics test lines WITH the micro lidar, so while it was failing those calibrations ran blind or fell back to defaults, and bad pressure advance gives exactly the observed pattern — a big solid disc sticks perfectly while thin isolated extrusions (tree feet, the purge line) lay down badly and lift.Plate 16, the bed map, is NO LONGER the next job** — it was queued on the assumption the failure was positional, and it is not.ON* and that part stands — switching it off kills flow calibration and first-layer inspection, and then nothing checks the installed plate against the slice. The restart count is history: the read went to 100% failure on 2026-10-09 before the swap.*

Filament (2026-10-07): 3 new Inland spools in hand — PETG+ Grey (the new cartridge material, replacing Bambu PETG Translucent in slot 3), PLA+ Raspberry (the scheme's red) and PLA+ Blue. Guide §2 rows 19–21.

Filament reality (2026-10-05): slot 3 is now Bambu PETG Translucent — dried and loaded, and plate 1 (cartridge parts + 4 rail stops) is printing in it on the Engineering plate. Still nothing loaded in ABS/ASA, PLA-HT, NylonX or PA-CF, so the rest of the set remains a PLA design print. The PETG cartridge run is the first PETG-on-PETG thread fit — yesterday's good fit was PLA-on-PLA.

🟡 Open design questions

  • ⏸️ WAND CATCH LOCK PIN, lying print — DEFERRED (operator via CTO, 2026-11-25): "low priority, strong enough, not load-bearing". The pin stays as released (standing, head down). The parked change is cad/freecad/parked/lumi_bigclaw_catch_wand_lock_pin_lying.patch (git apply it to lumi_bigclaw_catch_wand.py; review runs with LUMI_NO_EXPORT=1). It does three things:

    • Prints the pin LYING on a 0.7 flat (−z, coplanar with the lug's face).
    • Adds a 2-pin TWIST COUPON (standing / lying in one socket block, the lug reacting the twist).
    • Adds rows: pry along the layers 30.9 MPa ≤ 33.3, against 24.8 across the layers standing; twist at the detent's 9 N·mm 0.43 MPa.

    Physics finding: lying does NOT cut the twist peak. A coin's 0.3 N·m gives 14.3 MPa on the layer through the axis (antisymmetric, no net force), against 12.2 standing on one whole layer. The coupon was to set that allowable.

    Open before resuming: the lying pin FAILs its print gate. It needs support: a 7.7 mm cantilever, a 1.7 mm one, and one steep face, from the D-shank's underside next to the flat.

  • ⏸️ BATTERY STRAPS — TABLED (operator, 2026-10-10): "very low priority". Do not spend time on it; parked here only so the measurement is not redone. The operator re-exported cad/reference/step/Base_plate_mounting_holes.step 2026-10-10 20:09 with the 4 strap markers, named Strap,Circle.001..004 — a COMMA, not a dot, which is why neither macro nor a Circle.-prefixed grep sees them. Marker Ø is 7.9–8.9, not 4.0 (harmless: the strap macro forces Ø4.0 and uses XY only, the plate macro hardcodes STRAP_HOLE_DIA = 4.5). ⚠️ That export is also RE-CENTRED: a pure X translation of −239.4307 mm, Y and Z untouched, proven on the 8 carriage markers common to both versions (spread 0.00e+00), geometry otherwise identical (3 solids, 858 faces). PSU_FALLBACK, BATTERY_BBOX and STRAP_FALLBACK are all pinned in the old frame, so anything that parses this export and mixes it with those pins lands 239 mm out. Strap XY, old frame (add −239.4307 for the new): 001 (174.1980, 23.5956) · 002 (171.7038, 110.5374) · 003 (285.0792, 110.1497) · 004 (284.8173, 24.1503). Left pair reproduces the pin to 0.06/0.30 mm; right pair is 1.665/2.427 mm lower in X and nobody has established why — if that is RIGHT_PAIR_X_NUDGE = -2.0 drawn into the model it must go to 0 or it applies twice. Not a clean −2.000 on both, so it reads as hand-placed. When this is picked up: CAD accepts the Strap,Circle.NNN name in both macros, and lumi_battery_strap.py needs the name filter lumi_base_plate.py already has (its regex matches the 8 carriage circles too and fails on the count).

  • 🚨 THE CARTRIDGE DOES NOT SLIDE OUT OF THE TAIL. (CAD, 2026-10-06, and true before v3 too — not caused by the growth.) There is no axial insertion path at all: the Ø66 caps cannot pass the Ø64 bottom neck or the Ø56 stop ring. The pack goes into an open shell half and the other half closes over it in Y. So a cell change means removing the retaining ring and tail ring and splitting the clamshell — not sliding a cartridge out. ⛔ Lumi_Wand_Flashlight_Variant.md says the pack "slides out through the tail cap". It does not. ⬜ Operator decision: is open-the-clamshell the intended service model? If cells were meant to come out the tail, this is a design conversation, not a fit problem — and it affects the whole "user-replaceable" premise.

  • ✅ Cartridge v3 fits — measured, not assumed (fit gate PASS 215/0, 2026-10-06). Zone 83.42 in 84.5 → 1.08 spare; every cartridge part clears both shell halves at 0.0000 mm³; cartridge lands on the Ø70/Ø64 step at Z 12 (204 mm² annular bearing), not the guide ring; stop ring Z 95.72..98.22 over a cartridge top of 95.42 → 0.30 mm lift when inverted, with 5.0 mm of Ø56-over-Ø66 overlap; 1.78 mm clear of the BMS pad. Wand↔︎extension joint unchanged — engagement 4.00, cradle 0.000 proud, all growth is inside the battery zone. ⚠️ The 4.00 is still a design number off the model, never measured on hardware (see PULL TEST).

  • ⬜ Verify on the first assembled v3 cartridge: do the solid rods still thread full depth? Same end geometry as the hollow rod that passed, but a solid core shrinks differently as it cools.

  • Extension LCD: LiLyGO or Waveshare? Both +Y halves are on plates (09 and 15) pending the facet dry-fit; the −Y half is shared.

  • Wand SKU: flashlight (retaining ring) or standalone (end plate + ring)? Building both needs a second end plate + end ring.

  • Should the 225° clocking tab be recessed? As built it stands 2.5 mm proud of the extension's flange face, which is the only reason the −Y half prints peg-end down instead of flange-end down like the +Y.

  • Adapter payload torque — NOT calculated. A mirrorless on the camera puck is not cleared. (creator/docs/Creator_Camera_Adapter.md §4)

  • Creator head CAD — architecture closed, macro not written. Pull LEDiL's free STEP models from Mouser first so the CAD carries the real optic solid.

  • Emitter bin — the bench star XMLDCL-C5AAAA1 is High-Density; production intent is HI-Blend. Re-confirm at MCPCB fab.

  • Retaining-ring infill is 40 % with a TODO: confirm still in the guide.

🔶 EBC covers: KINK FLUSH CUT (operator 2026-11-23, both covers; reprint needed) Operator photos 2026-11-23: blobs and strings along the bottom of the grille where every fin meets the lower wall, one fin broken on the right cover; the left showed the same, milder ("you may want to look at fixing ... so they print right"). Cause (CAD, measured on the exports): the outer wall's top stood 0.27 PROUD of the slant's outer face along the whole kink. The trim that removes the wall above the slant has its rail B EBC_TRIM_BURY (0.15) outside the wall while the slab's rail B is EBC_RAIL_BURY (0.1) inside, so the trim plane tilts off the slab plane and leaves a lip. Flipped, that lip is a down-step whose first layers start in air at every fin root (a 243 mm² steep face growing from a free edge, printgate.steep_unsupported). The two covers are exact mirrors and slice identically (LumiAI 3DPrinter); the right only looked worse. Fix (lumi_ebc_vents.py, KINK FLUSH CUT): before the slab fuse, the wall over the slab's own outer surface less EBC_KINK_UNDER 0.02 is cut away, built triangle by triangle from the slab's rails (rail B carried 3 mm further out, so the 0.1 strip outside it goes too), clipped to the wall's band; the panel side is untouched. Moving the trim's rail instead was tried first and shifted the hip geometry (a new 0.74 mm² floating region), so it was dropped. Gates, old → new, both covers: the kink face 243 mm² → gone; floating regions 0 → 0; cantilever / bridges / curved 0; solids diff: nothing added, 48.2 mm³ of lip removed (x 7.8–11.9, z 18.6–23.9 in the print frame, along the kink to both taper ends); sections at Y −50 / −47 / 19 / 22 / 25 flush. Bambu Micro Lidar (Dual Red Laser) X1 series 1 ✅ FITTED 2026-10-09 — LID002 · Bambu Lab US · order us783634741743058945 · $65.24. Marker read worked first try. ✅ OLD LIDAR SERIAL, read 2026-10-09 off the removed module: 00E09B3C. ⚠️ IT DOES NOT MATCH THE PREDICTION. The repair log says this build's lidar SN reads 00ExxA; this one has a B where that rule puts the variant letter. Do NOT conclude the machine was dual red — the build is confirmed single-red by DIRECT OBSERVATION (a blue laser visible during the first-layer scan, 2026-10-05; the dual-red build shows two red dots and no blue), and an eyewitness beats a parse. ⛔ The conclusion is that the 00ExxA/00ExxB rule, taken from a third-party article, is NOT RELIABLE as we recorded it — the serial is 8 characters and we never established the field layout. Do not buy or reject a part on that rule again; identify the variant by the laser colour during a scan.
TH Board Set V9 (Dual Red Laser) X1/X1C 1 ✅ FITTED 2026-10-09 — DLB014 · PartsBuilt 3D · order 71435 · $69.84. The V9 set is both boards, so the lidar, the interface board and the toolhead board are all new. ⚠️ A dual-red lidar and a dual-red board are a matched pair and replace a single-red pair wholesale — that is why both were ordered, and the fitted machine now works. ⛔ The serial rule once quoted here (00ExxB dual → 003xxC/D/Y; 00ExxA single → 003xxA/B) FAILED ITS ONE TEST: the removed lidar reads 00E09B3C, a B, on a build confirmed single-red by direct observation. Treat the rule as unreliable — see the lidar row above.

The combo includes an AMS 2 Pro and a Textured PEI plate, so the plate bought 2026-10-07 becomes the spare. 📦 PROD project file for the H2S: cad/print/lumiAi_Complete_PROD_H2S.3mf (2026-10-14, byte-copy of lumiAi_Complete.3mf). It still carries the X1C printer block inside — on first open in Bambu Studio, switch the printer to the H2S 0.4 and SAVE, which rewrites it as a real H2S project. The two Inland user presets (PLA+/PETG+) are printer-agnostic (inherit Generic, no compatible_printers restriction) so they survive the switch; they are also exported to cad/print/profiles/*.json for re-import on any machine. ⛔ The paragraph above first claimed the presets were printer-agnostic — WRONG (2026-10-14, operator observed them greyed under "unsupported" after the switch): Bambu Studio scopes a user preset through its base, and Generic PLA/Generic PETG are X1/P1-family instantiations. Fixed with dedicated Inland PLA+ @H2S / Inland PETG+ @H2S user presets inheriting Generic PLA @BBL H2S / Generic PETG @BBL H2S (same operator-calibrated deltas, arrays widened to the H2S's 3-nozzle shape, X1-only filament_extruder_variant key dropped) — in the workstation's Studio user folder and in cad/print/profiles/. On opening the PROD file, pick the @H2S variants in the filament dropdowns. ⛔ Everything below is the decision record that led here and is kept for the reasoning, not as an open question. The buy-window, the H2S-vs-H2D comparison and the cancelled MatterHackers demo are all settled.

⚠️ First-print observations to record on first prints on THIS unit (it is staying — the exchange was reversed 2026-10-14; answers not yet taken): whether the H2S has a lidar (third-party source said no; Bambu's own page 402s), what its toolhead and plate-detection behaviour is like in use, and whether the X1C's 100%-failure plate-marker history is a model-level weakness or was one bad unit. Second machine also means the single-printer bottleneck is gone — the wand halves are ~5 h each and there are two of them, so halves and rings can now run in parallel.

Decision record — why the H2S, kept for reasoning only6–8 weeks**, tied to production ramp — so the old late-December review would have

landed AFTER the purchase.that demo is CANCELLED** (below). The decision point is now the H2S pickup at Micro Center, 2026-10-10 10:30, and whatever the H2S shows in use after that.*

Default is the H2S. Real AMS-combo pricing, Micro Center Tustin 2026-10-07: H2S AMS Combo $1,399.99 (25+ in stock) · H2D Combo 1, 749.99 * *(11instock)—a * *350 gap, not the $300 the bare-printer figures suggested. Both combos include an AMS 2 Pro AND a Textured PEI plate, so the plate bought today becomes the spare either way.

📌 The AMS 2 Pro DRIES filament, and that matters here more than it would elsewhere — this project has already lost a spool to embrittlement, needed 6 h of external drying before PETG could be loaded, and carries NylonX that re-absorbs in 1–2 hours. Drying in the feed path is a real operational win, not a spec line.

✅ BUY FROM MICRO CENTER — 3 YEARS of cover, more than buying direct. They add their own +1 year on top of Bambu's 2 (operator confirmed at the store, 2026-10-07). Micro Center is on Bambu's authorized reseller list, so the 2-year holds (their own page says 1 year — that is their generic listing, not Bambu's terms). Keep the receipt; claims go to Bambu, returns in the first 15 days go to the store. Single nozzle, 340×320×340 — the largest dedicated single-nozzle volume in the line — 350 °C, PMSM servo extruder with clog detection, 23 sensors.

⛔ DEMO CANCELLED 2026-10-09 (operator). Read "HANDS-ON DEMO BOOKED: 2026-10-20, MatterHackers facility, H2S and H2D" with a six-item checklist until then. The operator has used them before — the X1C purchase — and got nothing useful: "never got any help from them ever." Micro Center is the channel. Their Tustin staff already supplied the real combo pricing, run an H2S on the floor at 1,500 h with no part replacements, demo the nozzle swap, and print PETG+ on it at home. Do not re-book a MatterHackers visit or write plans that depend on one.

The questions that demo was meant to answer are still open and now go to Micro Center, most of them at the 10-10 pickup: whether a hardware revision has shipped since the early H2 batches; what toolhead and lidar failure rates they see across the H2 line; whether the $300 / 3-year plan survives a claim; and — confirm rather than assume — that the H2S has no lidar (third-party source; Bambu's own store page 402s).

Re-evaluate the H2D at the 2026-10-10 Micro Center pickup, not in December. Buy it only if the 2026 field reports have cleared; otherwise H2S. What H2D buys over H2S is the second nozzle — a dedicated support material, which is a real win here because PETG welds to its own supports where PLA releases. What it costs is a failure mode H2S does not have. Check each of these before switching:

Sources to re-read: H2 issues thread, owner report, long-term review.

🚨 X1C RELIABILITY TALLY, 2026-10-08: 2 print heads · 6 extruder teardowns · 1 failing lidar — in ~33 MONTHS of ownership and UNDER 500 PRINT HOURS. Bought 2024-01-30; the operator's summary is "it's been like this for 2 yrs" — there has not been a good stretch. That is not a wear curve, it is a defect rate: a hardened nozzle should outlast 500 hours comfortably and a lidar should last the machine. ⛔ Calling it "wear" excuses it, and I did until the operator pointed out the hours.

📌 The failures cluster in the FILAMENT PATH and the SENSORS, not in motion or print quality: AMS jams and cross-winding, extruder clogs, the lidar. Worth carrying to Micro Center at the 10-10 pickup — ask what toolhead and lidar failure rates they see across the H2 line. ⛔ Said "the 2026-10-20 demo" until 2026-10-09; that demo is cancelled. Whether Bambu improved those parts matters more here than build volume or price, and it is the question the board had missing.

📌 TOOL-LESS NOZZLE SWAP, and on this bench that is not a convenience. Measured history, not a guess: 2 head-nozzle replacements and 5 extruder teardowns in 18 months on the X1C. And it will not stop — LumiAI runs carbon-filled NylonX, which is abrasive by design, so frequent nozzle changes are inherent to the material set rather than bad luck. The H2 series' 1-clip quick-swap hotend compounds across the machine's life in a way a spec sheet does not show. (Operator, after handling one at Micro Center 2026-10-08.)

📌 PLAN: a DEDICATED NOZZLE FOR NYLONX on the H2S (operator, 2026-10-08). This is the quick-swap argument's real payoff, and it closes a documented failure rather than reducing wear. Guide §9: "PLA snaps ~10 in into every load after a NylonX print" — HMS 0700-8003, nylon residue plugging the hotend because PLA at 220 °C cannot remelt nylon that melts at 260–290. Two nozzles means the two materials never share a melt zone and that class of clog stops existing. ⚠️ Only practical BECAUSE of the 1-clip swap — on the X1C it is a teardown each way, which is exactly why it was never done.

📌 The H2S is the NEWER design; the H2D came first (Micro Center specialist, 2026-10-07). That matters: the H2D's field complaints belong to the earlier generation, and the H2S arrived later with a LARGER single-nozzle volume at a LOWER price. Buying the H2D would be paying more for the older design.

⛔ Not the H2C ($2,199+). Its Vortek 6-hotend swapper exists to kill purge waste on multi-colour prints; LumiAI segregates colour by plate and prints none. 59 sensors against the H2S's 23 is the wrong direction after two print heads and a lidar in ~33 months.

📌 Either way the bed grows to ~340×320, so the 3MF plate layout gets re-consolidated on arrival — the rings (287 mm) and the two fence halves (288.8 mm) are split only because they exceed 256. Expect ~14 plates → ~11. ⚠️ BED = 256.0 is a single scalar in both build_lumiai_complete_3mf.py and verify_3mf.py and assumes a square bed — it has to become BED_X / BED_Y.

The X1C stays as machine 2 once the lidar is in, so this purchase buys capability, not redundancy.

🖥️ Who is doing what

Machine Role
GmkTek (Cursor, 8 sessions) real FreeCAD regens (~/.local/bin/FreeCAD-weekly-2026.11.11 only), the fit gate, check_exports_fresh.py, printing, bench measurement — the only box where those run
Windows (2 sessions) docs, the Bambu project, firmware. ⛔ cannot run the export freshness check (no OCP)
Cloud design, CAD under the OCC shim, docs, gates, git. No LAN, no hardware

Agents by area: CAD, 3D Printing, Electronics, CNC, Marketing, Ultra (Halo bring-up), Fleet, ROSMaster.

FreeCAD builds on the GmkTek (operator 2026-11-16, via the CTO): one build at a time. CAD runs every build except the cradles. 3D Printing runs the cradle builds (the BigClaw cradle family, with the catch / A-back gate) and prepares the print books; on CAD's parts it rebuilds the books in Python and runs the 3MF and mesh gates. Karma checks the books only, with no FreeCAD. CAD's FreeCAD gate results travel in its push message. CAD's full-assembly run, its cradles stage included, is CAD's: it hangs the cradle parts at HEAD and does not rebuild them.

There IS a live channel. Sessions reach each other by name (ListAgents / SendMessage) when Remote Control is on — Windows ↔︎ GmkTek is bidirectional and proven. Cloud sessions are one-way: they receive but cannot reply; their answer appears in their own transcript at claude.ai/code. Replies arrive on their own — do not poll.

Pull before you reason, push when you decide, and update this file in the same commit.

⚠️ 2026-10-03: two CLAUDE.md files were written independently four minutes apart, by two sessions that could not see each other — the exact failure this board exists to prevent. They have been combined into one. Then this file was created twice the same way. If you are about to create a file that "every session needs", git fetch first and check whether another session already made it.

Recently decided

  • 2026-11-24 (operator via Electronics-Assembly + CTO; CAD): Ultra takes the BAY v3 set (fence, bars + tray, carrier) on its own base plate with its PSC-100. No plate holes are needed for the set: the fence posts match, the tray is located by the bay, and the bars screw to the fence. The PSC-100 moves 13.0 mm back (X unchanged), so its terminal strip sits where the PSC-150's does: its back face is 5.6 from the pack, the same as the PSC-150, and the deck slot (Y 25–45) and the buck are unchanged. The operator marks through the PSU's holes and drills the 2 new ones by hand; the model puts them at FILE (190.36, −84.43) / (272.61, 39.57). One route set serves both PSUs (the same terminal line). The carriage screw under the PSU (hole (196.37, −82.00), 6.48 c-c from the new front PSU hole) is a non-issue: the rail carriages use flat-head (countersunk) M3s that sit flush (operator). Carriage holes are countersunk by hand for flat-head M3 (the operator); the plate model and the NC cut plain Ø3.4 through-holes.

  • 2026-10-27 (operator, observed): the NylonX→PLA purge is PRINTER-SPECIFIC; the guide had one procedure for both. On the H2S the built-in filament-change routine does it correctly by itself — it holds ~270 °C (inside NylonX's 260–290 °C melt range) through the unload AND the purge of the new filament, which is exactly what the manual procedure exists to force. So on the H2S: use the built-in routine, do not drive temperature by hand; the check is that the purge runs clean — no black streaks or CF flecks — and a cold pull is optional, only if specks persist. The manual 280 °C hand-feed is the X1C procedure and stays mandatory there: the confirmed failure (~8 mm blob, every PLA load snapping at ~10 in, full extruder teardown) is dated 2026-06-24 — months before the H2S existed, so it is an X1C incident. The HMS 0700-8003 troubleshooting row is now scoped to the X1C, with a note that the same symptom on an H2S would mean its change routine is NOT covering the swap and should be reported. ⬜ Untested: whether current X1C firmware now behaves like the H2S — until the operator says so, drive it by hand.
